Correlation alone is insufficient; repeated pair searching creates selection bias.

ST035 — Sector-ETF residual mean reversion. Evidence R for the studied approach [S16]. Markets: equities with appropriate sector proxies; scope: days. Estimate lagged factor exposure, model residual deviations, and form constrained positions. Include hedging, turnover, and borrow. A stock-specific event or changing factor relationship can make an apparently cheap residual rational.

ST036 — PCA residual mean reversion. Evidence R for the studied approach [S16]. Markets: a sufficiently broad liquid equity universe; scope: days. Fit factors using past data, construct residual signals, and rebalance under exposure constraints. Track component and universe changes. Statistical factors, crowding, and transaction costs can shift; published results are not stationary constants.

ST037 — ETF creation/redemption arbitrage. Evidence C [S17]. Markets: eligible ETFs and underlying baskets; scope: execution-dependent. Compare executable basket value with ETF price after all fees, financing, and hedging. Direct creation/redemption requires the relevant authorized-participant access. A retail premium/discount trade is a separate, unhedged or imperfectly hedged hypothesis, not this mechanism.

ST038 — Dual-listed or ADR relative value. Evidence H for the selected pair and rules. Markets: verified equivalent claims; scope: minutes to days. Confirm conversion rights, share ratios, currency hedges, settlement, and simultaneous tradability before comparing executable prices. Exit on convergence or constraints. Nonconvertibility, borrow recalls, taxes, holidays, and transfer delays can preserve discrepancies.

E. FOREIGN EXCHANGE

ST039 — Currency carry. Evidence R [S04, S18]. Markets: eligible currency instruments; scope: weeks to months. Define exposure using tradable forwards or actual broker financing, rather than policy rates alone. Rebalance on schedule with currency and funding caps. Exchange-rate moves, crowded exits, and liquidity shocks can exceed accumulated interest.

ST040 — Cross-sectional currency momentum. Evidence R [S19]. Markets: currencies supported by the chosen data and instruments; scope: months. Rank past currency excess returns and construct permitted winner/loser exposures. Use executable conversions and rollover. Published evidence includes limits to capture; turnover, spreads, funding, and crowded reversals must be measured.

ST041 — Currency valuation or PPP reversion. Evidence H for the proposed valuation measure. Markets: currencies with appropriate economic data; scope: months to years. Specify the valuation model, release lags, and a slow rebalance rule. Structural inflation differences, capital controls, and long-lived misvaluation can overwhelm a simple fair-value estimate.

ST042 — Dollar-carry timing. Evidence H for the proposed timing rule. Markets: explicitly specified currency baskets; scope: weeks to months. Separate dollar exposure from carry ranking, define the state variable and hedge, and evaluate each source of return. Exit by rule or risk limit. A profitable generic carry study does not establish a dollar-timing model.

ST043 — Triangular FX arbitrage. Evidence H for an executable strategy; a conversion identity alone does not establish attainable profit. Scope: one fully specified three-pair cycle. Use simultaneous directional bid/ask quotes, available size, fees, and attainable latency. Execute only with an approved legging policy. Stale prices and incomplete fills can turn a displayed discrepancy into outright currency exposure.

ST044 — FX session-range breakout. Evidence H. Markets: individually evaluated currency feeds; scope: intraday. Define the session in exchange-aware time, freeze a range, and trade subsequent breaks with rollover and news filters. Exit before a specified cutoff. Daylight-saving changes, dealer spread behavior, and overlapping sessions must be part of the rule.

F. CRYPTO SPOT, FUTURES, FUNDING, AND VENUES

ST045 — Crypto time-series momentum. Evidence R for the study's sampled cryptocurrencies and period [S20]. Markets: individually eligible liquid tokens; scope: source-aligned daily or longer research. Use lagged returns and a separately specified execution rule. Require survivorship-aware listings and venue data. The study does not establish identical behavior in every token or on five-minute charts.

ST046 — Crypto cross-sectional momentum. Evidence R [S21]. Markets: point-in-time eligible token universes; scope: periodic ranking. Form exposure from lagged relative returns, with liquidity and concentration limits. Measure shared crypto-market and size exposure. Listings, failed assets, fabricated volume, and unavailable shorting can materially change implementable results.

ST047 — Dated-futures cash-and-carry. Evidence R for the documented crypto carry research [S22]. Markets: eligible spot and matching dated futures. Compare basis with financing, fees, margin, and custody costs; size a contract-correct hedge and manage it to a defined exit or expiry. Interim margin calls, basis widening, venue failure, and settlement mismatches remain possible.

ST048 — Spot/perpetual funding carry. Evidence H for the proposed strategy; related carry research [S22]. Markets: eligible matching spot and perpetual products. Hedge delta and compare expected funding with all holding costs under conservative scenarios. Reassess each funding interval; exit on inversion, hedge failure, or limits. Funding is variable, and perpetual basis need not converge on a known date.

ST049 — Cross-venue crypto arbitrage. Evidence R for observed segmentation and trading discrepancies, not frictionless profits [S23]. Markets: approved venues with pre-positioned inventory where needed. Compare executable buy and sell prices after fees and rebalancing costs. Transfers are not assumed instantaneous. Withdrawal restrictions, collateral differences, counterparty risk, and failed legs can prevent capture.

ST050 — Triangular crypto arbitrage. Evidence H for the executable strategy. Markets: three compatible pairs on an eligible venue. Calculate the complete bid/ask conversion cycle with quantity steps, depth, fees, and residual dust. Cancel or hedge incomplete cycles according to policy. Displayed prices without available size are not actionable arbitrage.

ST051 — Crypto ETF/spot or ETF/futures basis. Evidence H for the executable strategy; ETF access mechanics [S17]. Markets: approved products with understood settlement and holdings. Specify currency, trading-hours, and hedge differences; compare executable prices, not an unverified indicative value. Premiums can persist, shorts may be unavailable, and retail accounts cannot assume creation/redemption rights.

ST052 — Liquidation-pressure response. Evidence H. Markets: derivatives venues with attributable liquidation and open-interest data. Define the event threshold and test continuation and reversion as different variants with fixed exits. Do not use retrospectively completed liquidation maps. Delayed feeds, liquidation clustering, exchange-specific coverage, and mark-price differences can invalidate the signal.

G. COMMODITIES, FUTURES SPREADS, AND RATES

ST053 — Commodity curve carry. Evidence R [S24]. Markets: specified liquid commodity contracts; scope: weeks to months. Rank a precisely defined term-structure measure using actual contracts, with fixed roll and rebalance rules. Distinguish spot moves, carry, and collateral return. Curve shifts, delivery constraints, and contract discontinuities can dominate a simple backwardation score.

ST054 — Inventory-informed commodity relative value. Evidence R for the documented inventory relationship, H for a new signal [S24]. Markets: commodities with reliable physical-inventory data. Combine timestamped inventory measures with a specified price/curve model and bounded exposure. Exit when the model or horizon expires. Releases are delayed and regional inventories may not represent deliverable supply.

ST055 — Commodity calendar spreads. Evidence C [S25]. Markets: eligible expiries of the same commodity. Define the relative-value thesis, trade the permitted near/far contract combination, and exit on target, time, or risk limit. Prefer supported spread orders. Calendar spreads still carry seasonal, liquidity, delivery, and outright price sensitivity when legs are not perfectly matched.

ST056 — Energy crack spreads. Evidence C [S46]. Markets: crude oil and refined-product contracts. Use documented units and a declared refinery-output ratio; trade deviations only under a separately evaluated model. Exit on normalization or invalidation. Regional quality differences, outages, transport, weather, and unequal contract liquidity can overwhelm the theoretical processing margin.

ST057 — Agricultural crush spreads. Evidence C [S47]. Markets: soybeans, meal, and oil or another explicitly modeled processing chain. Convert units correctly and specify the processing ratio before constructing a spread. Trade only a validated valuation or momentum rule. Input/output location, seasonality, crop shocks, and mismatched legs prevent automatic lock-in of an industrial margin.

ST058 — Seasonal commodity spreads. Evidence H. Markets: commodity contracts with a plausible recurring production or demand cycle. Specify the contract-month pair, entry calendar, holding period, and information available at each date. Count each season as dependent evidence where appropriate. Weather shifts, policy changes, sparse observations, and repeated calendar searches can create false patterns.

ST059 — Intercommodity relative value. Evidence H for the model; spread mechanics [S25]. Markets: economically related but nonidentical commodities. Estimate a historically specified hedge, constrain residual risk, and trade deviations with a relationship-break exit. Substitution can change; unit conversion, energy content, geography, and evolving supply chains matter more than a superficially correlated chart.

ST060 — DV01-matched yield-curve steepener/flattener. Evidence C [S26]. Markets: supported government bonds or rate futures. Define which maturity spread should widen or narrow, then size opposing legs by instrument-specific rate sensitivity. Recompute after material changes. Parallel-rate neutrality is approximate; curve twists, convexity, financing, and delivery-option changes remain.

ST061 — Yield-curve butterfly. Evidence H for the proposed implementation. Markets: three defined maturity points. Establish duration or factor-matched wing and belly weights and trade a specified curvature signal. Exit on normalization, deadline, or exposure breach. Hedging a parallel move does not remove slope, convexity, liquidity, or model-estimation risk.

ST062 — Treasury cash/futures basis. Evidence C [S27]. Markets: approved Treasury cash bonds and corresponding futures. Model delivery eligibility, conversion factors, accrued interest, financing, and cheapest-to-deliver behavior. Trade only after executable cash and futures quotes and margin stress pass. Repo repricing, liquidity demands, delivery options, and crowded unwinds can create substantial losses.

H. OPTIONS AND VOLATILITY STRUCTURES

ST063 — Covered call. Evidence C [S28, S29]. Markets: owned eligible underlying shares and properly sized calls. Specify strike selection, expiration, sale timing, and assignment handling; exit or roll under an evaluated rule. Premium reduces some cost but does not remove stock downside. Upside is capped, and early assignment can change dividends and holdings.

ST064 — Cash-secured put. Evidence C [S30]. Markets: eligible underlyings the account can fund upon assignment. Reserve the required cash, sell the defined put, and track expiration or early assignment. A high premium is not free income; a large underlying decline can produce substantial losses. Do not silently convert this into leveraged short-put exposure.

ST065 — Protective put. Evidence C [S31]. Markets: a defined underlying position plus compatible long puts. Specify hedge fraction, strike, tenor, and replacement schedule. Evaluate the insurance objective and ongoing premium cost. Protection depends on coverage and contract terms; gaps between expiries, exercise mechanics, and mismatched settlement can undermine the intended floor.

ST066 — Collar. Evidence C [S32]. Markets: owned underlying, a long put, and a covered short call. Match quantities and define expiration, strikes, and assignment policy. Evaluate downside protection alongside capped upside and costs. A nominally zero-premium collar is not cost-free and can create operational complications when one leg is exercised.

ST067 — Bull call debit spread. Evidence C [S33]. Markets: compatible calls on the same underlying and expiry. Buy the lower strike and sell the higher strike using a supported combination order when possible. Define thesis, exit, and expiration handling. An intact standard spread's payoff is bounded; legging and assignment can create different temporary exposures.

ST068 — Bear put debit spread. Evidence C [S34]. Markets: compatible puts on the same underlying and expiry. Buy the higher strike and sell the lower strike, then manage the complete spread under predefined rules. Evaluate net debit, liquidity, and payoff. A correct bearish direction alone may not cover premium, timing, and execution costs.

ST069 — Defined-risk credit spreads. Evidence C [S35]. Variants: bull put and bear call spreads, recorded separately. Sell one option and buy a farther out-of-the-money option with compatible terms. Require portfolio loss limits and reliable combined execution. Credit collected is not expected profit; gaps, expiration, assignment, and closing liquidity require explicit handling.

ST070 — Iron condor. Evidence C [S36]. Markets: compatible put and call credit spreads. Define wing width, placement, expiry, profit/exit rule, and aggregate Greeks. Measure losses under directional jumps and volatility changes. Four legs increase friction and operational complexity; a high proportion of small gains can coexist with poor overall returns.

ST071 — Long straddle or strangle. Evidence C for the straddle construction [S37], H for each forecasting rule and strangle adaptation. Buy the specified call and put combination before a defined movement or volatility thesis. Exit by thesis, time, or risk budget. Realized movement must overcome premium and costs; time decay and volatility repricing matter.

ST072 — Calendar or diagonal option spreads. Evidence C for the documented calendar [S38], H for the chosen diagonal and timing rules. Specify all expiries, strikes, underlying exposure, and front-leg management. Evaluate term-structure and Greek changes across scenarios. Different expiries introduce path, assignment, and volatility risks that a simple expiration payoff chart does not capture.

ST073 — Dispersion/correlation trading. Evidence H for the proposed implementation. Markets: an eligible index and a sufficiently representative constituent-option basket. Specify which volatility/correlation exposure is sought and hedge aggregate sensitivities. Keep research-only without institutional-quality chains, execution, and risk tools. Single-name jumps, changing weights, discrete dividends, and many-leg costs can defeat the model.

ST074 — Bounded volatility-risk-premium strategy. Evidence H for the proposed trade; variance-premium research provides context only [S39]. Markets: explicitly chosen listed options with bounded exposure. Compare a defensible volatility forecast with executable option pricing, then select a permitted structure and exit rule. Equity-return predictability research does not prove that this short-option strategy earns net profits.

I. BONDS, CREDIT, AND EMERGING-MARKET DEBT

ST075 — Bond carry and roll-down. Evidence C [S40]. Markets: eligible cash bonds or carefully specified substitutes; scope: weeks to months. Estimate coupon/accrual and movement along an assumed unchanged curve, net of financing. Rebalance by schedule and risk. A curve shift, default, call, or liquidity shock can outweigh modeled carry and roll-down.

ST076 — Corporate-credit value. Evidence R [S41]. Markets: bonds with reliable issue-level prices and issuer information. Rank spread compensation relative to a declared fundamental/default-risk model, controlling duration and liquidity. Exit through scheduled reranking or invalidation. Wide spreads can reflect genuine distress; stale marks and trading costs can distort an apparent bargain.

ST077 — Credit momentum. Evidence R for the studied style [S40]. Markets: supported corporate-bond universes; scope: months. Define whether momentum concerns total returns, excess returns, spreads, or linked equity information; evaluate each separately. Rebalance with turnover limits. Sparse trading, price marking, issuer events, and rate exposure can produce misleading signals.

ST078 — Credit carry. Evidence R for the studied style [S41]. Markets: eligible corporate bonds; scope: months. Measure specified spread and carry relative to duration, expected losses, and funding, then diversify issuer exposure. Exit by schedule or risk trigger. Larger yield is not larger assured profit; defaults and correlated liquidity stress can overwhelm accrual.

ST079 — Defensive credit. Evidence R for the studied style [S41]. Markets: eligible corporate bonds; scope: months. Define the quality, maturity, leverage, or risk features used and distinguish them from valuation. Construct a constrained portfolio with an explicit benchmark. Defensive selection can underperform in risk rallies and remains exposed to rate, liquidity, and issuer risk.

ST080 — Emerging-market sovereign bond styles. Evidence R for the source's hard-currency debt research [S42]. Markets: eligible sovereign bonds and supported hedges; scope: months. Specify value, momentum, carry, or defensive variants with country caps. Do not infer local-currency results from hard-currency evidence. Restructuring, capital controls, political events, and sparse quotes require separate modeling.

J. ADDITIONAL EQUITY SIGNAL SPECIFICATIONS

ST081 — Accrual quality. Evidence H [S50]. Markets: equities with point-in-time accounts; scope: months. Rank low versus high working-capital accrual intensity using a versioned accounting definition. Specify publication lags, missing-tax treatment, and sector exclusions before formation. Rebalance on a fixed schedule. Accounting restatements, mergers, thin trading, and correlated value exposure can distort the result. The reviewed code is a research construction, not a verified executable strategy.

ST082 — Residual momentum. Evidence H [S49]. Markets: equities; scope: monthly. Estimate returns unexplained by a declared market-factor model using past observations, then rank standardized residual strength. Define estimation and ranking windows, skipped observations, and exposure limits. Rebalance when scheduled rather than every tick. Model changes, factor crowding, turnover, and unstable residual estimates can overwhelm a gross signal. The metadata method was inspected; the corresponding Python path was unavailable.

ST083 — Distance from the 52-week high. Evidence H [S51]. Markets: equities; scope: weeks to months. Rank closeness to a trailing high using only information known at formation. This is a cross-sectional ranking, distinct from buying an intraday high breakout. Specify corporate-action treatment explicitly: the inspected code and metadata differ on adjustment descriptions. Resolve that discrepancy before promotion. Define reranking exits and liquidity limits; sharp reversals remain possible.

ST084 — Customer-to-supplier information diffusion. Evidence H [S52]. Markets: equities with historical customer links; scope: monthly. Use lagged returns of disclosed major customers as a candidate predictor for supplier returns. Freeze relationship membership at its public availability date and reject ambiguous company matches. Exit on scheduled reranking or link invalidation. Stale disclosures, look-ahead in entity matching, concentrated customers, and common industry exposure are principal failure modes.

ST085 — Piotroski-style financial-strength selection. Evidence H [S53]. Markets: value equities with complete accounts; scope: months. Form a documented multi-component score of profitability, financing, and operating improvement within the declared valuation universe. Preserve the original universe constraint when attempting replication; broad-universe adaptations are different versions. Define missing-component handling and periodic exits. Statement revisions, sector accounting differences, and distressed-stock trading costs can change conclusions.

ST086 — Net payout yield. Evidence H [S54]. Markets: equities; scope: months. Compare dividends and repurchases net of equity issuance with a declared lagged market-value denominator. Use actual reported activity rather than assuming an announced repurchase will occur. Define exclusions, currency treatment, and rebalance dates. Exit on reranking or data failure. Financing changes, one-off distributions, and value traps can produce misleadingly attractive scores.

ST087 — Net share issuance anomaly. Evidence H [S55]. Markets: equities; scope: months. Rank lower versus higher historical issuance using point-in-time share counts and a documented split convention. Reconcile the metadata and code adjustment conventions before claiming replication. Distinguish compensation dilution, acquisitions, and financing transactions. Use scheduled reranking and borrow checks. Small-stock exposure, buyback timing, and distressed issuers can dominate realized outcomes.

ST088 — Analyst earnings revisions. Evidence H [S56]. Markets: equities with licensed estimate histories; scope: weeks to months. Rank changes in comparable forward earnings estimates after their actual publication. Match forecast fiscal periods so a year rollover cannot masquerade as a revision. Define zero/negative denominators, coverage requirements, and expiry. Exit on reranking or invalid estimates. Vendor revision histories, low coverage, and rapid information incorporation are important limitations.

ST089 — Short-interest information signal. Evidence H [S57]. Markets: equities with timestamped short-interest reports; scope: weeks to months. Evaluate reported short interest relative to available shares as an information signal, with a predeclared direction. Publication date differs from measurement date. Do not automatically reinterpret high short interest as a squeeze-buy signal. Rerank after fresh publications; borrowing costs, recall risk, stale reports, and squeezes can invalidate the trade.

ST090 — Public spin-off event selection. Evidence H [S58]. Markets: publicly traded spin-offs; scope: months after eligible separation. Build a point-in-time event universe, track parent and child identities, and define a finite holding age and liquidity screen. Use public corporate-action terms to handle distributions and delistings. Exit at deadline or invalidation. Forced selling is a hypothesis, not a guaranteed catalyst; leverage, business quality, and sparse new-company history matter.

K. ADDITIONAL OPTIONS STRUCTURES

ST091 — Debit butterfly. Evidence C [S59]. Markets: compatible listed options; scope: a specified expiry. For the call version, buy lower and upper strikes and sell twice the middle strike, with equal wing widths. Put implementation belongs to the same payoff family. Define target, debit ceiling, and early close policy. The expiry payoff is bounded, but assignments, pin risk, and broken-leg execution need separate handling. No price-target accuracy is implied.

ST092 — Short iron butterfly. Evidence C [S60]. Markets: compatible listed options. Sell a call and put at a common middle strike and buy protective outer wings. Record relation to ST091: a closely related expiry-payoff family, with different funding and exercise mechanics. Size from complete-position stress and define closure before uncertain assignment. Credit is not profit; commissions and movement away from the center reduce the economic case.

ST093 — Long iron butterfly. Evidence C [S61]. Markets: compatible listed options. Buy the center call and put, then sell farther outer call and put wings at the same expiry. This expresses a sufficiently large movement while capping the payoff. Define maximum debit, event timing, and close deadline. Link it to the same butterfly family for dependency counting. Timing error, premium decay, and assignment can defeat the thesis.

ST094 — Debit condor. Evidence C [S62]. Markets: compatible calls or puts with four ordered strikes and one expiry. The call implementation buys the two outer strikes and sells the two inner strikes. Require matched wing widths for the standard bounded structure. Link it to the condor family including ST070, rather than counting it as independent alpha. Define net-debit and exit rules; four-leg liquidity and assignment costs matter.

ST095 — Reverse iron condor. Evidence C [S63]. Markets: compatible put and call debit spreads. Buy an inner put and call and sell the farther outer wings, seeking a sufficiently large move before expiry. Record its relation to ST070 and ST094. Evaluate both break-even regions and combined costs before entry. Exit as a complete structure by deadline; restrained movement or poor timing can lose the premium.

ST096 — Call ratio backspread. Evidence C [S64]. Markets: compatible same-expiry calls. Sell one lower-strike call and buy two higher-strike calls for a strong upward-move thesis. This is the long-ratio orientation; do not silently reverse it into uncovered short-call exposure. Evaluate the loss trough around the long strike, net premium, and assignment path. Close by a specified deadline and prevent repeated averaging into the trough.

ST097 — Put ratio backspread. Evidence C [S65]. Markets: compatible same-expiry puts. Sell one higher-strike put and buy two lower-strike puts for a large downward-move thesis. This is the downside implementation of the backspread family. Model the intermediate loss region, assignment funding, and the underlying price floor. Gains on ordinary equity puts are finite as price approaches zero; never label them unlimited. Close the entire position by policy.

ST098 — Synthetic long equity exposure. Evidence C [S66]. Markets: matching listed call and put. Buy the call and sell the put at the same strike and expiry to create stock-like directional exposure. Treat this as an implementation and financing alternative, not new alpha. Reserve settlement capacity and account for dividends, interest, and exercise style. Exit or roll under a declared policy; a small initial premium does not imply small economic exposure.

ST099 — Synthetic short equity exposure. Evidence C [S67]. Markets: matching listed put and call. Buy the put and sell the call at the same strike and expiry. Treat this as a short-exposure implementation with potentially unbounded loss as the underlying rises. Keep excluded from routine live eligibility unless the account policy explicitly permits this exposure and its margin management. Assignment, distributions, financing, and squeeze risk require full-position monitoring.

ST100 — Covered ratio stock repair. Evidence C [S68]. Markets: an existing eligible stock holding plus compatible calls. Buy a lower-strike call and write twice as many higher-strike calls, with the holding covering one short call and the long call covering the other. Quantity matching is mandatory. This changes future payoff and caps recovery upside; it does not erase the original loss. Require an explicit exit horizon and assignment plan.

L. CRYPTO AND ON-CHAIN RESEARCH EXTENSIONS

ST101 — Inventory-aware two-sided market making. Evidence H [S43]. Markets: eligible crypto or equity order books; scope: seconds to minutes. Quote bid and ask around a declared reference, skewing prices and size with inventory, volatility, and measured fill risk. Cancel stale quotes and impose hard inventory and order-rate limits. Source S43 supplies a theoretical model, not a current venue edge. Queue priority, adverse selection, outages, and fees can overwhelm spreads.

ST102 — Concentrated-liquidity provision. Evidence C [S69]. Markets: explicitly supported AMM pools and chain deployments. Allocate liquidity to a defined price interval with declared fee tier and capital budget. Track token inventory, fee accrual, and whether liquidity is active; define repositioning or withdrawal rules. Fee income must be evaluated against inventory losses, gas, and adverse selection. The protocol mechanism alone provides no profitability evidence; contract and token behavior require separate review.

ST103 — Hedged AMM liquidity provision. Evidence H [S69]. Markets: a supported liquidity pool and a separately executable hedge instrument. Combine the ST102 inventory with a bounded, periodically adjusted hedge derived from actual pool state. This is a strategy variant within the liquidity-provision family. Define hedge thresholds, funding assumptions, and withdrawal conditions. Discrete rehedging, basis shifts, chain delays, liquidation, and divergent venue availability can leave substantial residual exposure.

ST104 — Unlevered on-chain lending allocation. Evidence C [S70]. Markets: individually approved assets and lending-pool deployments. Supply owned assets under fixed protocol and asset caps, and monitor utilization, withdrawal availability, and realized interest. Define exit conditions before allocating. This is a yield-allocation mechanism, not a directional prediction. Variable rates, smart-contract failure, collateral loss, frozen liquidity, and asset depegging can outweigh accrued interest. No recursive borrowing is implied.

ST105 — Protocol liquidation execution. Evidence C [S70]. Markets: explicitly supported protocol deployments. Observe eligible positions, calculate collateral received against debt repayment at current deployment parameters, and submit only when simulated transaction economics exceed costs and the approved reserve. The liquidation trigger and bonus are contract-specific. Gas competition, oracle changes, failed transactions, and collateral price moves can erase an apparent spread. Never hardcode one close factor for every deployment.

ST106 — Staking with a price hedge. Evidence H [S71, S72]. Markets: approved proof-of-stake assets with reliable validator or staking access and an eligible hedge. Compare expected rewards with hedge funding, fees, and capital usage. Maintain a bounded hedge against actual exposure, and plan withdrawal delays before entry. This adaptation is not established by staking documentation. Slashing, validator downtime, price-basis mismatch, and hedge liquidation can exceed rewards.

ST107 — Staking-token redemption discount. Evidence H [S71]. Markets: a separately specified liquid staking token and underlying asset. Research whether an executable discount covers fees, redemption timing, and risks; enter only after the actual token protocol and redemption path are documented. Ethereum withdrawal documentation supplies background only and does not certify any token. Exit through a verified redemption or market sale. Queues, depegging, contract exposure, and hedge costs can prevent convergence.

ST108 — Public on-chain flow signal. Evidence H. Markets: crypto instruments with reliable public chain data; scope: hours to days. Define exchange-address evidence, confirmation depth, a net-flow measure, and a directional hypothesis before evaluation. Use only address labels known at the time; internal transfers must not be treated as customer demand. Exit on signal age or invalidation. This is an unverified research proposal: attribution error, reorgs, wash activity, and anticipation can invalidate it.

M. ADDITIONAL INSTITUTIONAL AND SPECIALTY METHODS

ST109 — Cross-currency funding basis. Evidence H [S73]. Markets: eligible spot, forward or swap, and funding instruments. Compare fully matched borrowing, lending, and currency-hedging cash flows using actual executable financing terms. Define collateral currency, settlement dates, and an unwind path. Institutional access may be necessary. Published parity deviations are not free money: balance-sheet constraints, liquidity, credit, and funding repricing can preserve or widen the basis.

ST110 — Swap-spread relative value. Evidence H [S74]. Markets: supported SOFR swap exposure and government-bond hedges. Size opposing legs by comparable rate sensitivities and evaluate the swap-versus-government spread after financing. Rebalance sensitivities and close at a declared target, deadline, or risk breach. Government specialness, collateral and settlement differences, convexity, and abrupt spread changes remain even when approximate duration is neutral.

ST111 — Short-rate meeting-path repricing. Evidence H [S75]. Markets: eligible short-rate futures or bounded options; scope: scheduled policy events. Translate settlement-period averages into a declared policy-path model using known meeting dates and published fixings. Enter only on a predefined discrepancy beyond costs. Exit after the event or model invalidation. Different averaging conventions, already-fixed days, risk premia, and surprise communication can make naive probability estimates wrong.

ST112 — Inflation-breakeven relative value. Evidence H [S76]. Markets: maturity-matched nominal and inflation-linked bonds with optional approved inflation hedges. Model real/nominal sensitivities, indexation lag, carry, and liquidity before expressing a breakeven view. Define convergence, deadline, and stress exits. Breakeven includes risk and liquidity components; it is not a pure forecast. Seasonality, deflation floors, funding, and imperfect maturity matching can overwhelm the thesis.

ST113 — CDS–bond basis. Evidence H [S77]. Markets: eligible bonds, credit protection, and financing; institutional scope. Match issuer, seniority, currency, maturity, and credit-event terms before evaluating a basis trade. Use executable rather than indicative spreads and define unwind costs. The cited study concerns sovereign markets; corporate transfer requires separate evidence. Documentation mismatch, recovery uncertainty, counterparty exposure, and financing stress make the position imperfectly hedged.

ST114 — Dividend futures forecasting. Evidence H [S78]. Markets: approved equity-index dividend contracts. Forecast the contract-defined dividend accumulation from point-in-time constituent information and compare it with executable pricing. Separate ordinary from special distributions according to contract rules. Exit on maturity, repricing, or forecast invalidation. Dividend cuts, index changes, currency conversion, and concentration create risks even if broad equity-price exposure differs from an index future.

ST115 — Equity-index cash/futures basis. Evidence C [S79]. Markets: an executable index basket or explicitly modeled proxy and its futures contract. Compare futures pricing with cash, financing, and dividends, then evaluate matched legs after full costs. A displayed index is not purchasable. Close through an executable convergence or roll policy. Tracking error, dividend surprises, borrow availability, asynchronous sessions, and many constituent fills can prevent capture.

ST116 — Energy calendar-spread options. Evidence C [S80]. Markets: specifically supported options whose underlying is the difference between two futures expiries. Define spread direction, strike, settlement, and curve-volatility assumptions; use bounded premium exposure unless another structure is authorized. This differs from ST072, which combines separate options with different expiries. Close by thesis or expiry policy. Storage shocks, nonlinear curve changes, and sparse spread-option liquidity matter.

ST117 — Power–fuel spark spread. Evidence H [S81]. Markets: compatible electricity and natural-gas contracts with region and delivery-period matching. Convert fuel using an explicit heat rate and compare generation margin with a declared model; account for emissions where applicable. Use sensitivity-aware sizing and a fixed exit rule. This models a product relationship, not guaranteed power-plant profit. Load shape, congestion, outages, efficiency, and physical delivery make mismatches material.

ST118 — Weather-index forecast trade. Evidence H [S82]. Markets: verified weather futures or options tied to a specified station and index. Translate timestamped probabilistic forecasts into the exact settlement statistic, with exposure bounded by forecast uncertainty and liquidity. Exit on information change or settlement policy. Historical city listings do not prove current trading access. Weather-model revisions, station anomalies, thin books, and basis to economic exposure can dominate results.

ST119 — Volatility-futures term-structure trade. Evidence H [S83]. Markets: eligible volatility futures of separately identified maturities. Trade a declared relative curve view with explicit sensitivity matching, stress scenarios, and maximum gross exposure. Define roll and close rules; do not assume a spot volatility index can be bought or that contango guarantees profit. Volatility jumps, maturity-dependent response, margin demands, and changing settlement expectations can rapidly widen losses.

ST120 — Machine-learning cross-sectional return ranking. Evidence H [S44]. Markets: equities with point-in-time feature histories; scope: periodic portfolio decisions. Train a versioned prediction model under strictly time-ordered internal evaluation, then form a constrained ranking portfolio after costs. Treat the model as a signal combiner, not an additional independent economic premium. Define drift suspension and scheduled refresh. Leakage, repeated selection, unstable features, and capacity limits can defeat published average relationships.

ST121 — Intraday noise-band momentum. Evidence H [S94]. Markets: liquid stock ETFs. Research candidate: compare current price with time-of-day movement bands, with a VWAP filter. Freeze band estimation, decision times, dividend handling, sizing, and session exits before testing. This proposed implementation is not a replication of the author method. Requires adjusted reference prices and timestamped intraday observations. Overlaps ST022; not an independent premium.

ST122 — Activity-filtered stock opening-range breakout. Evidence H [S95]. Markets: exchange-listed stocks. Research candidate: apply an opening-range rule only to unusually active securities. Fix the range, relative-volume denominator, eligible universe, ranking timestamp, and costs before testing. Never rank with the completed day’s future volume. Requires historical listings, corporate actions, and executable quotes. Extends ST021; published family evidence does not validate this implementation.

ST123 — Multi-horizon crypto channel ensemble. Evidence H [S96]. Markets: liquid crypto. Research candidate: combine channel signals and volatility-aware allocation across a historically reconstructed universe. Freeze horizons and turnover constraints before evaluation. It extends ST003 and ST045. Source horizons must be preserved; a five-minute adaptation needs separate evidence. Historical liquidity ranks must include later delistings. Not implemented in Batch 01.

ST124 — Fast-signal execution-timing overlay. Evidence H [S97]. Markets: liquid intraday instruments with a defined parent strategy. Research candidate: use a short-horizon signal to time eligible parent orders. Compare against identical parent decisions without the overlay, including missed trades, delay, spread, and adverse selection. This is an execution overlay, not a separate alpha claim. Exact timing and fill rules remain to be specified and tested.

5C-RESEARCH. PUBLISHED EQUITY-SIGNAL INDEX — 212 SOURCE KEYS

This is a discovery index of the 212 records classified as Predictor in the inspected Open Source Asset Pricing metadata, not 212 additional verified trading systems. It deliberately excludes 114 Placebo and 5 Drop records from the active candidate list. The author project's classification is provenance, not this project's independent endorsement. Each entry still needs full-method extraction, point-in-time inputs, executable portfolio rules, costs, and internal evaluation. Some are alternate measurements, conditional variants, or already covered by ST001–ST124.

Source: Chen and Zimmermann, *Open Source Cross-Sectional Asset Pricing*, and the author's [metadata file](https://github.com/OpenSourceAP/CrossSection/blob/master/SignalDoc.csv). The [release page](https://www.openassetpricing.com/data/) describes historical datasets; these are not live market updates. The inspected repository is GPL-2.0 licensed; inspect the applicable licenses and underlying data rights before reusing code or redistributing data. This document includes identifiers and bibliographic facts, not a copy of the implementation code or detailed source definitions.

Use the source key as the stable external identifier. RP numbers are local display references only. Resolve source keys against the metadata rather than assuming every key has a Python file of the same name. Source code and descriptive metadata can disagree: the High52 and ShareIss1Y adjustment conventions require reconciliation, and the attempted ResidualMomentum Python path was unavailable. Preserve these issues in the registry.
